ParlaMint-DK_20160419130248	No amendments have been tabled. Does anyone wish to speak? Sir. Nikolaj Villumsen, please.
ParlaMint-DK_20160419130327	Today is a sad day, a day when Denmark is once again entering a failed war. It is a war that, in the worst case, can destabilise a totally destroyed Syria and a chaotic Iraq further. This is not a discussion about whether or not Denmark should fight ISIL. Of course we must fight ISIL, but when Enhedslisten is so fiercely opposed to the government's proposal, it is because the proposal is exactly the recipe for another failed Danish war. The government has obviously not learned from the brutal experiences of the wars we have waged on the Danish side; the brutal experiences of the war in Afghanistan, where the experience is that the civilians killed by Western bombs led to a strengthening of the Taliban; the experience of the war in Iraq that bombs led to the chaos from which ISIL has grown. If Danish bombers hit civilians in Syria and Iraq, we risk strengthening ISIL. And to make it even worse, this time the government will give Danish fighters the opportunity to bomb even closer to inhabited areas with great danger of civilian loss. It could be brutal. It could cost civilian lives. And this can lead to the strengthening of extreme forces. In the Enhedslisten, we would much rather be fighting ISIL, but we will not do so by throwing bombs that risk hitting civilians and which risk increasing their support for extremists. Instead, we will strengthen the local democratic forces in Syria by working to involve them in the peace process and to increase aid for humanitarian reconstruction in the areas that they have liberated from the Assad regime and from ISIL. Together with the Alternative, we have put forward a proposal on this which will be dealt with later today in the Danish Parliament, and I hope, of course, that the Danish Parliament will support those proposals. With this proposal, the government is opening up the way for prisoners to be extradited to the Iraqi authorities without any agreement being reached that they will not be tortured or executed, despite the fact that we know that the Iraqi authorities are guilty of systematic abuse of prisoners and the use of the death penalty. Such extraditions are likely to be contrary to Denmark's obligation under the Antitorture Convention, and both Amnesty International and DIGNITY have alerted the Danish Institute Mod Tortur to the Danish Parliament. The government will send Denmark to war on a very, very thin basis. The government has no idea what the consequences of the Danish bombings in Syria will be. If ISIL is hunted on the run from an area, there will be a risk that the Assad regime or extreme Islamist groups will take control. It is most absurd, however, that we are in the totally grotesque situation, that the Minister for Defence has not wanted to give us an insight into which Syrian groups should have military support from Denmark. We have been told that these are individual groups, but we have not been given the full picture. I have asked the Minister for Defence and his answer has been quite clear: I cannot tell you who we are going to war with. We know that Danish fighters will have to help selected groups in Syria with air support, but we cannot get full insight into which groups are involved. We know that parts of the coalition support extreme Islamist groups inside Syria, but we do not know whether Denmark should support these Islamists. It should be logical that we cannot combat ISIL by simply supporting another group of extremists. And after previous Danish war participation, we should all be extremely concerned about what forces Danish war participation can risk strengthening this time. I must honestly say that I simply do not think it can be right for the government to ask the Folketing to support a war in Syria without wishing to give the Folketing a full insight into who it is that Denmark should give military support inside Syria. But from here I have to say that it becomes even more grotesque, because not only will the Minister for Defence not tell who Denmark is going to cooperate with, but the Minister for Defence will not even answer whether he can vouch for the groups that Denmark is going to provide air support. In other words, we are in a situation where the Secretary of Defense asks the Danish Parliament for support to go to war with military groups, for which he would not even agree with the Danish Parliament himself. Honestly, what is it that the Secretary of Defense and the government have to hide? I think it's completely grotesque. And even more grotesque is the fact that the Secretary of Defense seems to have support from the Danish Parliament for his misguided, secretive and clearly ill-thought-out warfare. I would have called on the Minister of Defence from the platform today to come to the floor and explain which groups should have air support inside Syria. As I said, I believe that we have a right to know before we vote in a moment. Who is it that Danish fighters must go to support inside Syria? The Secretary of Defense did not go to the platform at first reading. The Secretary of Defense did not want to answer in committee whether he could vouch for groups that need Danish air support. Now we have a situation in which the Minister for Defence is not present in the Chamber at all. I assume, of course, that the Minister for Foreign Affairs can give a clear answer. I assume, of course, that the Minister for Foreign Affairs will take the floor. And I would like to urge the Foreign Minister to answer whether the Foreign Minister can vouch for the groups that the Foreign Minister and the Government are asking the Folketing to provide military support in Syria. I would ask the Secretary of State to answer why it is that the government has so far wanted to keep this hidden. I think the government and the Minister for Foreign Affairs owe us an answer to what groups the government and the Minister for Defence have not yet wanted to stand by.

They are also attacking in Europe. They hate the West, they hate us, and it's not something we can talk to them about. It's been tried. Should we then turn our backs and leave them alone and hope that it will pass and that we can change some socio-economic factors so that they may not hate the West in 50 years' time and commit terrorist attacks against Western countries and Western citizens? No, I don't think so. I think that we should do what we can to actively combat them, to combat their having a sanctuary in the Middle East from which they can send their terrorists against us. I think we should back our traditional allies who want to take that fight now. I think we should show the new style that Denmark has shown after the Cold War, where we actually dare to make a difference in the world. We dare to back up our allies and be there where it's difficult and where it's dangerous. We should not do as we did during the Cold War, where we plunge our necks and are free to see other people defend our security and security. We ourselves must help to make Denmark safer, and we make Denmark safer by fighting the possibility of establishing a territory in the Middle East where they can commit their atrocities in peace and quiet and send their terrorists against us. So I am proud that the Danish People's Party will vote in favour of the resolution. I am proud that Denmark will take an active part in the fight against Islamic State, for this makes Denmark safer. Thank you.

As I said, I am explaining the position of the entire government. Let me just try to turn the time back 20 months. ISIL's terrors and horrors were so small in the face of the world community. Terrible videos appeared about wars, about struggles, about refugees who could tell about ISIL's barbaric methods, mass executions, the use of rape, oppression of peoples; the Yazids have been mentioned several times as one of the groups that were really being slaughtered by ISIL. After some quick victories and a very strong momentum, ISIL was in a situation where they were so strong in Iraq that there was real concern about whether it would collapse. Today we are in a completely different situation. Thanks to the efforts of the global coalition, we are in a situation in which Iraq has not collapsed, where, on the other hand, ISIL is being pushed back, and we are doing so, because in Denmark, together with more than 65 coalition partners, we have taken responsibility. It stresses that the fight must not be simple, easy or short-lived; we must be prepared for it to be long and complex on the contrary. That is why the coalition has a broad spectrum and multiannual strategy. There is now a need for Denmark to reaffirm and strengthen its support for the efforts of the international coalition, and we want Denmark to assume that responsibility on the part of the government. This is partly because there is momentum in the fight against ISIL. They've lost 40%. of the area occupied by them at the time their distribution was highest in Iraq, they have lost 10%. the area occupied by them in Syria and they have reduced their oil revenue because efforts have been made to remove the oil installations, the means of transport they used for their illegal oil sales. We also believe that it is important from the Danish side that we listen to it when our close allies, in this case France and the United States, have asked us to consider whether we can strengthen our contribution. We take that seriously and we take it with us. And then we may do it most of all, because the price of not acting now can be too high. It is unacceptable that terrorist movements occupy large rural areas so close to Europe and force millions to flee. It is unacceptable that terrorist movements, as ISIL has done, should be able to reach out and plan from their territories and hit us in the West, in Europe, in the United States, but also in the Middle East, in Asia, in Africa. If you take the map of where either ISIL departments or people inspired by ISIL have hit with terror, you can see that the world as a whole is under attack from ISIL. This is not an easy decision. There I agree with the many rapporteurs who have said that. It is never an easy decision to send Danish soldiers to war, but it is a necessary decision. Neither I nor the others in the coalition have any expectation that we can resolve the conflict by throwing bombs alone, but everyone in the coalition agrees that we will not solve the conflict without military force. I think it was sir again. René Gade, who said wars are ending at the negotiating table. Yeah, that's right. But the question is, what brings the parties to the negotiating table? And a party that, like ISIL 20 months ago, only saw victories would never sit at the negotiating table. A party that sits at the negotiating table is a party that knows that you cannot win any more on the battlefield. If we fail to act militarily, ISIL will simply expand their caliphate with incalculable humanitarian and security policy consequences. That is one of the reasons why the motions for resolutions we will be discussing later today are inadequate. Following on from the press stories of the last few days and today's debate, there are two things I would like to make quite clear. F-16 broadcasts to Iraq and Libya in 2011. Both from Denmark and from the coalition, there has been a great focus from the start on dealing with the laws of war and on protecting the civilian population in both Iraq and Syria. We're not going in that direction. There is a direct answer to Mr. Martin Lidegaard's question no new line in relation to previous motions for resolutions. Secondly, the clear point of departure for the handling of possible detainees is that there can only be transfer under an agreement with the Iraqi Government guaranteeing that the detention is dealt with in accordance with the conventions, humanely, and that the death penalty is not executed. The government will do everything we can to ensure that it is enforced every day. Having said that, I would also like to say quite clearly that ISIL cannot be defeated by military means alone. That is also why the motion for a resolution lists a large number of civilian interventions that are being taken into account. There is a need to take action on all fronts, both the hard and the softer. That is correct, and we cannot give answers to all the groups with which we have had to work, among other things. because they have not gone through the process of approval, but we have said, in return, that there will be a process of investigation and approval, in which we will go in and look at what kind of cooperation relations they have had, what management structures there are, prior to making the extension of whom we should work with, if necessary, when we do not talk about the Iraqi army, the Iraqi militias, who are, after all, associated with the Iraqi Ministry of the Interior, or the Kurdish Peshmerga forces up from the northern part of Iraq. It is clear that we need to look into it so that we know who we are approving before we start working together. That is what has been proposed, and that is what we have been informed of. Then I would like to make a few comments on the speeches that have been made. First of all, I do not think it is a proper description that there is no clear objective in relation to what action should be taken. The aim is initially to reduce Islamic State, ISIL, as much as possible. That is to displace them from the land area they have, knock them off the rails so that they are no longer a threat, either to the Iraqi people or to the Syrian people, to turn them back so that they do not have an area where they can plan their many different terrorist attacks from. That is the strategy. At the same time, we are working to rebuild the land that is being liberated. At the same time, we are working to stop the sources of funding that have been, including illegal oil sales, including the transfers of funds that have been made. At the same time, we are working to stop the recruitment base both in relation to foreign fighters coming from Western countries such as Denmark, Belgium, France, those coming from the Arab world from countries such as Tunisia and those coming from the rest of the world. We are working with a large number of countries to stop young people being radicalised before they are radicalised. We work with a number of countries to tell about the experiences we have, positive experiences, what is called the Aarhus model or the Copenhagen model, to stop young people before they are radicalised. We are working with a large number of other countries to stop the propaganda machine that ISIL has created, which paints a picture that they are Islam's real version, and to document what they are actually doing around. We have a broad-spectrum approach to this, and we place emphasis on both the military and the civilian. I have mentioned to Mr. Nikolaj Villumsen that Britain, thankfully still away is an active part of the alliance. That is the basis we want to give our soldiers, quite clearly. Let me just say, also to anticipate later questions, that it is the case that if our soldiers have a situation where prisoners have been captured, we have just established a task force in Copenhagen, consisting of the Ministry of Defence, the Ministry of Foreign Affairs and the Ministry of Justice, to decide what should happen, so that it is precisely they who are responsible for what decisions are made. As part of the motion for a resolution, we are sending 20 soldiers to help with such a guarding task if it were to be the case that, against expectation, we went and took prisoners of war. So we have taken all the necessary steps we can take to avoid getting into a situation where our soldiers have a case they cannot deal with on the ground. To those who, like Mr. Holger K. We are active in Iraq because we are invited by the Iraqi army and the Iraqi Government to help them in their defence of Iraq. We are also active in Syria because it has been shown that Syria has neither the ability nor the will to stop the use of Syria by ISIL to attack Iraq. We're not in a military conflict with Assad. We are not in a military conflict with Syria, but we are engaged in a political process which, this week, ran its third round in Geneva on negotiations to create a political transition in which Assad will be removed as the leader of Syria, where new decent conditions will be created and democratic elections will be created so that the people of Syria can choose for themselves who will lead Syria forward. When we help to repress ISIL from an area, it is clear that when we talk about Iraq, it is the Iraqi army, the Iraqi community, that must take responsibility for the areas. To the extent that we had to help in Syria to bring the ISIL back, it is of course our view that those with whom we work and with whom we have approved, together with the Syrian civil defence organisation Syrian Civil Defense, are the ones who should have the highest possible responsibility for what happens in the area afterwards. We cannot leave the responsibility behind and expect others to lift it if we do not contribute ourselves and therefore Denmark contributes. We're already contributing. With this resolution, we are contributing even more.

ParlaMint-DK_20160419175154	I would like to ask you to remain calm in the Chamber. Please leave the Chamber in good order so that there will be sensitivity for the remaining ones. The debate is open and I give the floor to the Foreign Minister.
ParlaMint-DK_20160419175235	It is a motion for a resolution which is, after all, somewhat on the same page as what we have been discussing, namely the question of whether the Democratic Union Party, PYD, and other secular groups should be involved in the peace talks on Syria. First of all, I would like to say that the conflict in Syria is a tragedy, first and foremost for the Syrian people, who have suffered for five years now from this extreme brutality and terror, for which the Assad regime and violent extremists in particular are responsible. Denmark has been active from the start of the conflict to help the Syrians and other civilians in the region. Few countries have made as much effort as Denmark. At the same time, from the outset, we have engaged in the fight against ISIL. Denmark contributes substantially, especially with the adoption we have just made, to achieving greater stability and peace in the region. The decision on the continued Danish involvement in Iraq and Syria to combat ISIL has now been taken with the adoption of motion for a resolution B 108. With the new resolution, Denmark will continue to make a solid military contribution and will also provide substantial support to the civilian area. This autumn, the government is launching a new Danish 3-year stabilisation programme for Iraq and Syria. Denmark will, within the framework of this programme, inter alia: continue to support the UN-led negotiations aimed at promoting a peaceful solution to the conflict in Syria, as we have done up to now. This is because the conflict in Syria is not just about ISIL. The main cause of the conflict is to a large extent the Assad regime, which in 2011 struck violently against peaceful popular protests. Since then, the Assad regime has fought the Syrian population with the greatest brutality and created a spiral of violence with tragic consequences for Syria and neighbouring countries. It must be stopped and a solution to the conflict must be achieved through political negotiations. On the part of the government, we, like the Enhedslisten and the Alternative, believe that the negotiations require sincere and active involvement of all relevant parties in the conflict, in particular from the Assad regime and the Syrian opposition. The essential point here is that it should be the Syrians themselves who decide who should represent them at the negotiating table. The best way to ensure a legitimate and sustainable negotiating group that can deliver the results we all hope for in order to achieve lasting peace in Syria is for them to choose their own representatives. It is therefore also positive that large parts of the Syrian opposition met in Riyadh in Saudi Arabia at the end of December last year. Here they agreed on the composition of the opposition delegation to participate in the forthcoming peace negotiations under the auspices of the United Nations. In this context, I noted that some Kurdish actors are actually members of the opposition delegation. This concerns, for example, KNC. The fact that PYD is not part of the Syrian opposition delegation is partly due to the fact that other opposition actors in Syria do not see PYD as a party working for the same goals as them. Nor has PYD itself moved closer to the Syrian opposition coalition, which is the internationally recognised representative of the Syrian opposition. In fact, we would like the Danish side to see the opposition reach agreement that as broad and representative a delegation as possible is involved in the peace negotiations. But it is the Syrians themselves who are responsible for that decision. We can't dictate anything to them. We must respect the opposition's right to form their own negotiating delegation. In recent months, Russia has been trying to interfere with the opposition delegation, among other things. by pushing to formally incorporate PYD into the negotiations. A large part of the other opposition sees it as an attempt to divide the opposition and thus weaken its position during the negotiations. We must be careful not to take the errands of other countries by pushing for the inclusion of individual groups in the negotiations. We are better served by supporting the overall peace process. We do best to support the work of the UN Special Envoy for Syria, Staffan de Mistura, in the negotiations. Denmark should not make this process more difficult. That is why we cannot support the motion for a resolution tabled by the Enhedslisten and the Alternative.

If a peaceful and stable future is to be created in Syria, it is, frankly, crucial that the moderate and secular parts of the rebellion be allowed to form part of the negotiations. Not only will it be forces that will speak for the same values of women's liberation and respect for minorities as the majority of this Parliament wants. They will also be representatives of the largest rebel groups. These rebel groups, which are currently being held out of Turkey, represent more men and women under arms in their armed forces than any other rebel groups do in Syria. They control larger areas of Syria than any other group does. Then some have come up with the bad excuse for not voting in favour of the proposal, that it is not at all something that the Folketing should be concerned with; that it is not at all something that should be discussed in this Chamber; that it is not at all something that we should be spending our time on. But that is not the case. The reality is that this proposal is deeply, deeply politically controversial. Because if this proposal is adopted, Denmark is going against the Turkish Government, against the government which is now bent its neck to prevent refugees coming to Europe. Denmark, the United States and the European Union have so far bowed to the demands of the Turkish Government to keep moderate rebel groups out of the negotiations, because the Kurds are a greater enemy of the Turkish Government than ISIL and Assad. The Ankara Government is frightened by the fact that there are religious and ethnic groups that can unite and demand equal rights. Why does it scare Ankara? This is because if it were to happen in Turkey too, the same thing would happen in the June elections, and Erdogan would lose his majority. Honestly, if we are to have peace, if we are to have stability in the Middle East, then we will not get it if we support oppressive regimes rather than local, secular and democratic forces. That is why we in the Group of the European People's Party wanted to make this proposal. That is why we are very pleased that the alternative has supported it, and that is why we will of course welcome it very, very much if the SF also votes for it. This will, of course, be done by addressing the United Nations negotiator and by the fact that the special envoy we have and working with the rebel groups in Syria, but so far have not wanted to touch the groups that Turkey does not like, with a ferocity, starting to work with them. Among other things, it will: In the context of our cooperation in the coalition, Denmark says to the United States that, of course, these secular groups must be part of the negotiations. It's not that hard. Of course, Denmark, both in coalition and bilaterally, as well as in the United Nations and in all other international assemblies, where it will make sense, can raise this issue. You can gather a circle of like - minded countries and work for it. Nielsen knows well, and our Foreign Minister knows that. That is why some parties do not want to vote for it. There is no doubt that President Erdogan and the king of Saudi Arabia would be enormously offended by this. On the other hand, there are some democratic forces who want to ignore ethnic and religious divisions, who want to gather together in a fight against ISIL and Assad, and who would be really, really happy. There is no doubt that these two despotic Islamists do not think it is good if secular democrats are allowed to be part of the decisions on a future in Syria, but I think we should work for it. And I don't think we'll have a lasting peace if we don't. So, yes, this proposal is deeply controversial. It is not because we think it should be, and it is not because it is weird or weird to discuss it. We have discussed the process in Syria on a regular basis in Foreign Policy Name and in the various committees of the Danish Parliament and in the Danish Parliament. We have had a position on whether Assad should be part of the peace process. We have had a position on whether Iran should be part of the peace process. And, of course, we can also have a position on whether PYD and other secular groups should be part of the peace process. Well, honestly, make some better excuses. What we are discussing today is whether we will do something actively to strengthen the peace process so that peace can be lasting so that it can be sustainable and that some secular groups can be involved. And frankly, we should not be discussing this in the Chamber today. It should be banal, it should be logical. Unfortunately, it is not, but I hope that one day it will become official Danish foreign policy.

ParlaMint-DK_20160419202610	Thank you. The idea behind this proposal is the idea and attitude that Denmark must make an effort in Syria, which makes a difference: Denmark can make a decisive difference in the fight against ISIL if we show that it is worth fighting against ISIL if we make it possible to rebuild, make a humanitarian reconstruction in the liberated areas where Assad and ISIL have been hunted on the run. Unfortunately, it is not possible everywhere in Syria today, where moderate rebels have succeeded in freeing the areas. Unfortunately, we have a situation in which there are large areas controlled by moderate rebel forces that do not receive humanitarian relief which do not receive humanitarian reconstruction material. It is not because there are no countries that would like to help them, for example Denmark, which would like to send mine clearance equipment to Kobane, but cannot be allowed to do so because the Turkish Government is blocking the border. So we have a situation where Denmark can make a difference if we actually bring out the emergency aid in these areas. Yes, it is clear that we, the Enhedslisten and the Alternative, on the part of the proposers, would like to rebuild Syria as a whole. As far as the Enhedslisten is concerned, however, I must also say clearly that when we have a situation where the Kurdish town of Kobane, which was liberated more than a year ago, is still in ruins, we also have a problem. If we imagine that right now we were all sitting in Raqqa discussing whether we should oppose the Islamic State, and then we could look forward to the day when we had taken a very, very hard and brutal fight and our houses and our homes had been completely destroyed, that one year after we still hadn't rebuilt our houses, then I think there would be a great deal among us that would say: No, then we wait just to take the fight. That is actually why it is quite appropriate to discuss how we can secure humanitarian reconstruction even in the cities that have stood up to ISIL. It's in Kobane. More than 200,000 of the city's inhabitants have now returned, but they cannot rebuild their houses. They cannot get reconstruction materials across the Turkish border. As has been discussed before in this Chamber, this is something we have actually discussed in the past. We had a question debate called by the Enhedslisten, which led to the previous government sending out mine clearance equipment. The problem is that this mine clearance equipment has not been able to come forward since the end of 2015, because Turkey closed the border. That is simply absurd. We often discuss the problems of Turkey keeping the border open for foreign warriors, keeping the border open for oil exports out of IS-controlled areas, but precisely here Danish mine clearance equipment cannot enter areas freed by moderate rebels. Fortunately, large parts of northern and northeastern Syria are liberated areas where secular rebels have hunted ISIL and Assad on the run. These are areas that house millions of civilians. These are the areas where we have the situation that we would very much like to help refugees from other parts of Syria, we would very much like to make it possible for the civilian population to stay, and we would also like to make it possible for refugees to return and build a future. I believe that Denmark should support humanitarian reconstruction and believe that it is time for action to be taken behind the much talk of aid in the neighbourhood. We of course believe that this should be done in Kobane on the part of the Enhedslisten, but we also believe that this should be done in other parts of Syria. This will not only mean fewer people being forced to flee to Europe. It will also strengthen local secular forces, and it will show the Syrian people that it is worth fighting against ISIL and that when your house is destroyed because you fight against ISIL, it can be rebuilt after liberation. I think it is positive that the Foreign Minister has announced that humanitarian action must be strengthened. I think it is positive that there have been positive indications from a large number of parties in the Folketing today that humanitarian aid should be increased and that we should talk about how we can help better in the neighbourhood. The problem is, after all, that there are large parts of the liberated areas in Syria that are controlled by moderate forces who have not received support from Denmark for the simple reason that it is politically impossible to bring it forward. That is why I think it is time that Denmark also dares to put an end to the anxiety that has sometimes prevailed in this House about the fact that the Turkish Government, which should also be a close ally, should be a partner in the fight against ISIL and should be a partner in the fight against people fleeing Syria, keeping the border closed. ParlaMint-DK_20160419203451	The debate has been opened, and the first rapporteur is Mr Seligman. Kasper Roug, Social Democrats. Here you go, sir. Roug.
ParlaMint-DK_20160419203512	Thank you for that, President. In order to expand its mining operations, Greenland repealed its zero tolerance policy in 2013 against investigating and extracting nuclear materials. The extraction and export of nuclear materials affects the entire foreign, defence and security policy of the Kingdom of Denmark, and therefore a need has arisen to establish a system of both international obligations and guidelines for control and transparency with all phases of both the extraction and export of nuclear materials from Greenland. The bill we are discussing here must form the basis for such a security control system, and it is based on a thorough Danish-Greenlandic preparatory work. Prior to the bill, a working group has been set up, consisting of Danish and Greenlandic authorities, to draw up a report on uranium extraction and export. Against this background, you have identified the need for legislation in this area. Subsequently, a joint declaration has been concluded between the Ministry of Foreign Affairs and the Department of Industry, Labour and Trade to establish a Greenlandic and Danish security control structure in mutual respect and equal value. It is all to ensure that the extraction and export of nuclear material from Greenland will take place in accordance with the highest international standards. The bill shall ensure that the powers necessary to comply with the international safeguards obligations of the Kingdom of Denmark and ensure that nuclear material can only be used peacefully and not in illegitimate weapons programmes or in connection with terrorism. Security checks shall include, inter alia: comply with the Nuclear Non-Proliferation Treaty, the IAEA Safeguards Agreement and the related Additional Protocol. The security control system proposed by the legislative proposal will correspond to the relevant parts of the European Atomic Energy Community Regulation on the application of safeguards. This applies today to Denmark, but not to Greenland. The proposed security control system will include: include a notification and reporting obligation related to possessing and exploiting nuclear materials. This includes, among other things: information sharing with the IAEA on nuclear material and installations, reporting and inspection access requirements and establishing the operation of an electronic accounting system for accounting and control of all nuclear material. A duty of supervision will also be introduced, and then the Ministry of Foreign Affairs will have to conclude bilateral agreements with the States to receive the nuclear material. The agreements will make a number of demands, including: legal, binding obligations to ensure that the material is used only peacefully. It will be the Ministry of Foreign Affairs, which will be the overall responsible authority for security control, and the Danish Emergency Agency, together with the Greenland authorities, will carry out the specific security control tasks. It is, for example, a question of carrying out accounting and control systems for installations and nuclear material in Greenland and of monitoring and checking compliance with the safeguards obligations, among others. through inspections by IAEA inspectors. With this bill and with the bill on the control of exports of dual-use products, which is presented simultaneously by the Minister for Industry and Growth, we ensure that future extraction and export of nuclear material from Greenland will take place under the strictest security conditions in the world. We also ensure that the material is used only for peaceful use. This is good for Greenlandic society, and a good agreement will be good for security and peaceful use. Social democracy supports the proposal.

ParlaMint-DK_20160419205032	Thank you. When the rapporteur of the Left in this area, Mr. Marcus Knuth, did not have the opportunity to be present in the Chamber, I hereby read his speech: With this bill Denmark wants nuclear material from Greenland to be exploited for peaceful purposes and that Denmark meets its international security obligations. When Greenland decided in 2013 to repeal its zero tolerance policy, this meant that the ban on the extraction and export of uranium and other radioactive substances was lifted. In other words, Greenland said good that uranium could be extracted in Greenland in the future. In order to examine the consequences of the lifting, a uranium working group was set up which stated in their report in October 2013 that a system was needed to implement the international obligations to carry out safeguards on nuclear material. Therefore, the bill is a follow-up to the uranium working group's report and the result of an agreement between Denmark and Greenland's Self-Government. As responsible for Greenland's foreign, defence and security policy, it is Denmark's job to ensure that the radioactive substance does not end up in dictators' weapons programmes or in the hands of terrorists. Denmark's international obligations derive primarily from the Treaty on the Non-Proliferation of Nuclear Weapons, the IAEA Safeguards Agreement and the related Additional Protocol. According to the conventions, Denmark is responsible for ensuring that uranium from the territories of the Kingdom community does not spread irregularly. The draft law will introduce a security control system in which notification and reporting obligations are linked to possessing and exploiting nuclear material. In addition, uranium exports will be made on the basis of a bilateral agreement concluded between the recipient State and the Ministry of Foreign Affairs. The agreement will involve a legally binding obligation on the recipient State to use only the nuclear material for peaceful purposes. The Left believes that it is necessary to get the agreement between Greenland and Denmark in place, and that is why the Left is voting in favour of the proposal.

ParlaMint-DK_20160419205734	Thank you. Uran mining in Greenland I see both proposals as very closely coherent, and therefore there will be a mix of the two proposals as I try to take them as a whole. Greenland has had a zero tolerance policy until 2013. It means it wasn't possible to dig for and sell uranium. Both in Greenland and Denmark there was unanimous support behind this position, but the new Siumutled Government has decided shortly after accession in early 2013 that they wanted the zero tolerance policy abolished. It was decided by 16 votes in favour and 15 against in the Greenland Parliament on a very, very small margin, I have to say, on such a serious issue. These are the legislative proposals that we have to take a position on today and we have to vote on the legislative proposals in the wake of the agreement. I would have recommended to the government that they had talked to the Folketing about it before they started making such agreements, so we were involved before the agreement was made. The reason why we have to take a position on this is precisely that there are essential security and foreign policy aspects to selling uranium because it can be used for nuclear weapons. Mining is an area that has been taken home to Greenland, but foreign and security policy are Danish areas of competence, which is why it is a duty that we have to take a position when Denmark is at risk of becoming a uranium-exporting state. We have always been a non-nuclear country, and we are now in danger of getting out of a completely different caliber of countries. We do not want to take that risk and we have three reasons for not voting in favour of the proposals. Firstly, we maintain many years of agreement on zero tolerance. Uranium must remain in the soil, it is toxic to humans, it is toxic to the environment, and it is dangerous to the safety of the planet. That is why the Commonwealth should not allow the export of uranium, and that is what the legislative proposals are primarily about and not about whether Greenland has the right to dig up here, of course they do. Secondly, it is not possible to ensure that uranium is used only for peaceful purposes. The reason is that the government, without asking the Danish Parliament, has made an agreement with Greenland that it is okay to dig for uranium. For Denmark, as a non-nuclear country, this means that it can only be used for peaceful purposes, which we have the experts' word for not being possible to ensure. If you read some of the articles written among other things. in the context of the lifting of zero tolerance in 2013, it appears that new figures from the International Atomic Energy Agency show that there is a large black market for radioactive material, including uranium. From 1993 to 2013, therefore, there have been 2,331 cases of illicit trafficking in radioactive substances, including uranium. Of these, 419 fall under the category of illegal possession and related criminal activity. 615 cases fall under the category of theft or loss, and 1,244 cases fall under the category of other illegal activity. Therefore, there must be a very, very strong control body if you are to secure against such things. And this has even happened in a market where there is a lot of control in advance. Although there are a large number of international conventions and rules to prevent crime, we know that uranium is being smuggled and that there is a large rogue market for trade in it. As things stand now, Greenland does not have the necessary expertise, neither does Denmark, in my opinion, and it lacks financial resources in particular to establish the necessary basis to protect itself and to match it here. Thirdly, in the Enhedslisten, we are also opposed to the use of uranium as energy for peaceful purposes. That is why we cannot support the two proposals.

ParlaMint-DK_20160419210900	Thank you. With the proposal, we ensure that the Kingdom Community can thus also comply with international obligations and rules on safeguards of nuclear material in Greenland for peaceful use. However, there are clear foreign and security policy aspects to what we have also heard in the past, as the Kingdom Community, with its adoption, could potentially become a safe exporter of nuclear material. It is therefore also very gratifying that Greenland's and Denmark's governments have recently reached an agreement on the very special conditions associated with this area, an agreement which, through close cooperation between Greenland and Denmark, will ensure the best possible control measures and tracking mechanisms in monitoring the use of any nuclear material. This follows up the recommendations of the uranium report and provides security for the framework for possible future handling and export of nuclear material from Greenland. The submission of the bill also gives me a good opportunity to call for the government's overall foreign policy strategy. As I see it, the bill here seems logical to me, in that the raw material area is a Greenlandic matter for which Greenland's Landsting and autonomy have taken on responsibility. This does not mean that we should become a chief judge in the Danish Parliament, but Denmark still has no responsibility for security policy, including ensuring that there is no proliferation of nuclear weapons. We have some international obligations to which we need to live up to. As I understand the bill, that alone is what is aimed at it. We have to make sure. We have a duty to do so. And then it must be so that it is adopted in the Folketing, of course, in the greatest possible respect that otherwise the raw material area is a Greenlandic matter. If there may be any breaks between the two considerations, the consideration of Greenland's autonomy and the raw material area and Denmark's responsibility for security policy, that is, of course, something we need to discuss. However, as a starting point, this seems to me to be a logical legislative proposal which we intend to support.

The security control system corresponds to the relevant parts of the European Atomic Energy Community Regulation on the application of safeguards currently in force in Denmark but not in Greenland. The draft law establishes a security control system in which notification and reporting obligations are imposed, which is linked to the possession and exploitation of nuclear material and which must be supervised. The Ministry of Foreign Affairs is overall responsible for security control in the Kingdom, but concrete security control tasks will primarily be carried out by the Danish Emergency Management Authority in close cooperation with the Greenland authorities. ParlaMint-DK_20160419221422	Thank you to the rapporteur. Here you go to the Secretary of State.
ParlaMint-DK_20160419221431	Thank you for a thorough review of the bill. Despite the thorough examination, I would nevertheless like to make a few comments on the background to the draft law, as various aspects of the draft law have been put forward by the rapporteurs. According to the 2009 Act of Self-Government, adopted by a very broad majority in the Folketing, Greenland took over the raw material area in 2010 and thus acquired the legislative and executive power in the area. Against this background, the decision to abolish zero tolerance for the exploration and extraction of uranium and other radioactive substances is a Greenlandic matter. However, since foreign, defence and security policy implications are linked to precisely extracting and exporting uranium, this is a landscaping that requires cooperation between Denmark and Greenland on how this can happen. Agreement was reached between the Government and the Home Rule Government of Greenland on this in a cooperation agreement at the beginning of this year. Two Joint Declarations have been concluded in the framework of the Cooperation Agreement: on safeguards and on export control of dual use products. Both security controls and export controls result from the Kingdom's international security policy obligations. The joint declarations constitute a political understanding between Greenland and Denmark on the practical implementation of the two legislative proposals we are discussing today. With the agreements, we are implementing concrete cooperation to ensure that Greenland can continue its efforts to expand its mining operations while, as a Kingdom, complying with our international non-proliferation obligations and meeting the highest international standards for uranium exports. The main objective is, of course, to ensure that uranium extracted in Greenland is used exclusively for peaceful and civilian purposes. This is a complex of agreements and legislation based on the division of competences within the kingdom, which clearly clarifies the division of responsibilities and tasks between Danish and Greenlandic authorities. There has been a close and constructive dialogue and constructive cooperation with Greenland and Nuuk throughout the period. I am pleased that we have managed to reach agreement with the Home Rule Government of Greenland on the legislative proposal before us today. This proves that together we can achieve results for the benefit of the whole Kingdom community also in difficult and complex areas. Let me highlight in particular four key elements of the rather technical draft law. First of all, there are security controls. The bill implements the international commitments for Greenland arising from the Treaty on the Non-Proliferation of Nuclear Weapons concerning the control of nuclear material. Security controls can be described overall as an accounting system that ensures that all nuclear material and related installations, equipment and activities are accounted for. Basically, it is about ensuring that nuclear material does not fall into the wrong hands. Secondly, there is the division of competences and cooperation with the national government. Nuclear material security controls are a security policy area linked to international non-proliferation obligations. This is a matter of common concern and this bill is being tabled for Greenland in the Folketing. Close cooperation with Greenland on the administrative management of security controls has been set up. Thirdly, legislation with relevant EU legislation is fleeing. We cannot simply apply the legislation in force in Denmark to Greenland. The reason is that the tasks relating to security controls in Denmark are mainly carried out through Euratom, the European Atomic Energy Community. As you know, Greenland is not a member of the European Union, nor is it a member of Euratom. It is therefore necessary to establish a special security control system for Greenland with this law. There is agreement with the national government that the law should correspond to relevant parts of Euratom rules. Fourthly, there are intergovernmental agreements on recipient states. In order to follow the highest international standards for uranium exporting countries, the draft law requires that exports of uranium from Greenland require a so-called intergovernmental agreement on recipient states. The overall demands that the Ministry of Foreign Affairs will make follow the highest international standards. This includes, among other things: compliance with international non-proliferation obligations and standards of physical protection as binding legal obligations in relation to the further management of uranium from Greenland. See, there have been a number of rapporteurs up here, but there are actually not so many questions left out, since the vast majority of questions have been about exactly the same thing. However, I will try to give Mr. Kasper Roug elaborates on § 10 (c). 2. Nah, sorry, it was DF's rapporteur, Jeppe Jakobsen, I have misquoted, I regret that. It has been a while since the parliamentary work started today. However, Article 10 (1) of the Directive does not apply. 2 the question of giving the Minister for Foreign Affairs an opportunity to grant derogations in specific cases. It is a question here that, for example, if you do not have the right to vote in local elections, you do not have the right to vote in local elections. If there is a lump of uranium or uranium material to be sent for study outside Greenland, but this is not a real start on exports, but a study alone, then it would be too big a device to set in motion such a full intergovernmental agreement simply to carry out a study on a small amount of uranium. That is why there is an opportunity for the Minister of Foreign Affairs to do so. The decision of the Minister for Foreign Affairs shall be made upon a specific request and the terms and conditions deemed necessary for compliance with the international security control obligations of the Kingdom of Denmark may be attached to the decision. The latter was a direct quote from the comments, page 26, first column. I hope now that I have shed a little more light on what this is really about. In addition, there have been two discussions. There I have much more respect for SF's attitude in this discussion. They say that you really disagree with what Greenland chooses to do, but that you respect that it is their right to do so. That was not my position, but my attempt to interpret SF's and Mr. Karsten Hønge's position on the matter. Although he is not present in the Chamber, I think he will be very much in agreement with that particular interpretation. I cannot understand those who say: Yes, we left it to Greenland to make the decision, but when they make a decision that we do not agree with, we want to block their ability to complete it. Nor can I understand those who say that it is a narrow vote or a narrow majority. They make a decision, and that is their right. They choose to do so, and we respect that. Then there are some areas that are the competences of the Commonwealth, and that is to ensure an export control that means that when it comes to uranium material, we meet the highest standards. We choose to do that with the bill here. The last question I choose to answer here is the question of the so-called haste. This is an agreement that we have spent a great deal of time with the Home Rule Government of Greenland to negotiate. It is an agreement in which the submission of the proposal today is in agreement with our colleagues in Nuuk. And when it is the case that it is due to enter into force on 1 July, it is because we expect it to be adopted in the Folketing before the Folketing takes a summer break around the day of the Constitution. Therefore, it is most natural that the law comes into force at the next change of months thereafter. There's no hurry, there's nothing urgent about this reading of the bill. It is time after we can get the opinion of the Home Rule Government of Greenland in preparation for the submission of a report to the Parliament's Greenland Committee here. And therefore it is in full agreement with and aligned with the treatment in Greenland. Thank you.

ParlaMint-DK_20160419223705	The debate is open, and that is Mr. Kasper Roug, Social Democracy. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20160419223725	Thank you. We have, of course, dealt with L 155 in the first reading earlier today, and a system of safeguards must be established to ensure that the extraction and export of nuclear material from Greenland is carried out in accordance with the highest international standards. We have heard that sometimes, it has to be said. Like L 155, this bill we are debating now stems from the work of the uranium working group and the recommendation that there is a need for legislation in the wake of Greenland's wish to extract and export nuclear material. Nuclear material also includes uranium and thorium, a material which has a dual use option. It can be used for both civilian and military purposes. The aim of the bill is therefore to introduce an export control system in Greenland that ensures that dual-use products, dual-use products, are not used for the development and proliferation of weapons of mass destruction or fall into the hands of countries that threaten international peace, security and stability. The draft law introduces an export control system corresponding to the relevant parts of the EU Dual Use Regulation, which currently applies to Denmark, but not Greenland, because Greenland is not a member of the EU. Specifically, the export control means that the export of dual use products from Greenland is subject to authorisation. The bill also requires permission to carry out uranium and thorium-containing minerals, including yellowcakes, as they are called, similar to the regulation in force in, for example, the United States, Canada, Australia and the European Union. The practical implementation of the export control tasks in Greenland will be carried out in close cooperation between the Greenlandic and Danish authorities, as reflected in the cooperation agreement on export control with dual use products concluded in January 2016 between the Home Rule Government of Greenland and the Danish Government. It is proposed that the Danish Business Authority should be the competent authority for export control of dual use products in Greenland and among other things. carry out tasks such as deciding whether to authorise or refuse applications for export of these products, update control lists if the EU makes changes, cooperate with the Ministry of Foreign Affairs, PET, FE and others on export control obligations in Greenland. In connection with the granting or refusal of an application to carry out nuclear material, the Danish Business Authority shall consult the Ministry of Foreign Affairs as to whether a so-called intergovernmental agreement has been concluded in order to ensure that the material is used only for peaceful purposes. If this is not the case, the export of the material cannot be authorised. The proposal also includes a ban on the export of dual-use products intended to be used in connection with weapons of mass destruction products. A fee scheme will be introduced to process an application for permission to carry out nuclear material from Greenland. The proposal on export controls and the Foreign Minister's proposal to introduce a security control system together provide an orderly, equal regulatory framework which guarantees that the extraction and export of nuclear material in Greenland will take place in accordance with the highest international standards. The Socialists support the proposal, and at the same time I must say that the Radical Left also supports Bill L 158.

It's gone out of a hole somewhere. There is, of course, a reason why some countries have been able to make nuclear weapons, even if we do not want them to. We close our eyes to some of it. If these are our friends in Israel, we'll close our eyes. If it's North Korea, we'll keep a close eye on it and think about where they got uranium. I think, like all the rapporteurs, that this is something that I do not have to take a position on, because it is Greenland's responsibility. No, it's not. ParlaMint-DK_20160419232709	The debate is open. And the first speaker is the Minister for Children, Education and Gender Equality. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20160419232737	Thank you. The Enhedslisten has tabled motion for a resolution B 83, which requires the government to put forward legislative proposals abolishing the possibility of setting up private care schemes and receiving subsidies for private care. Specifically, the Enhedslisten, with its proposal, wishes to repeal the current Chapters 14 and 15 of the Daily Offers Act. And the party states in its observations that a change to the law must have a forward-looking effect. This means that private care systems already in place will be able to continue during an undefined transitional period. The unit list shall state the reasons, inter alia: The proposal states that private care is not subject to the same requirements as day-to-day care and that the increasing number of private care services restricts the management possibilities of municipalities. I can say that the government sees this as rather different. For the government, it is important that there is a high degree of freedom of choice in the day-to-day provision area; that parents should experience flexibility and choice when finding care for their child. And that is why it is also important that there is a diversity of offers in this area. The basic thing about free choice is that you have the option of choosing something other than what you are offered if, for various reasons, you need a different solution • a solution that suits your family better. Parents often place emphasis on different things when choosing one offer rather than another. Some parents attach great importance to the fact that the offer is close to home, while others stress that it is close to the parents' workplace. Some stress that there are extended opening hours. For others, it is more important that the offer has a special profile... it can be focused on outdoor life, body and movement, an athletic children's garden, a focus on music or something else entirely. That is why it is also positive, we in the government think, that today there are a number of different options for finding a place for your little child, for example. it is possible to point out to the institution that you as a parent want in your municipality and that you can also choose, as a parent, to have a place in a day offer in another municipality, a place in a private institution or subsidies for various forms of private care, if you do not want to make use of the offers to which the municipality may assign. For the government, private care schemes and private care subsidies are important elements of free choice. Private care is not the same as a day offer. There are different content requirements for day care, but for some parents, the possibility of receiving private care grants is an alternative or a supplement, which is what makes the best sense in their daily life. There may be many different reasons why parents say no to the municipality's offer of a place in a municipal or self-serving day offer and instead choose to get subsidies to hire a private fitter or pay for a place in a private care scheme. For some parents, this may be due to the fact that there is no day offer that is near the family home and that matches the wishes that you have. Others choose to receive the grant for a temporary period until they can be accommodated in a particular day-care institution in which they want their child to go. Finally, there may also be parents who have unequal working hours and who therefore need care at times outside the normal opening hours of the day offer. These are just a few examples of different families and different reasons why you need a free choice and a flexible choice. If you ask the parents who make use of the scheme, I am quite sure you can get even more examples of why they choose to use the free choice and flexibility. In essence, I do not really think that it is the individual cause that is important either. It is that each family can choose what is right for them and is right for their children. In the government we stress that parents have the opportunity to choose different offers and we respect the choices made by each parent. And basically, we believe that both freedom of choice and diversity, including in the area of children, are very important to ensure that there are also good offers for all children. At the same time, I would like to make it clear that we in the government are constantly focusing on how we can develop the day-off area. That is why we have among other things. taken the initiative to set up the Quality Forum for Day Offers, where we, together with stakeholders in the field, discuss selected topics and relevant topics on how we can develop our day offer area and how we can ensure that there is a good framework both for the play, learning and care that children should have as part of a day offer or in a care scheme. ParlaMint-DK_20160420002438	Thank you. I think it is paradoxical that, for a long time, we have experienced two, so to speak, opposing trends when we are talking about the area of children. One trend is that you are very concerned about how we can detail the area on all heads and edges. Not only are we concerned with the large framework, but we want to regulate how the educational work is to be carried out, if not in the smallest detail, but rather in detail and well described in the legislation. And the other trend is to say that we can also run day-to-day offers where there is no regulation at all. I think it is running somewhat together and I think that there is a tendency for not all the rapporteurs who have spoken, but I hear it in some places that the same people who think it is really important that we have an extremely detailed management of how to work pedagogically in some day-to-day services, at the same time have the impression that we can also operate something where there are no real demands. I find this extremely contradictory, and that is what we in the Enhedslist have wanted to challenge with this proposal. We find it extremely problematic that when the public pays about three quarters of the cost of private carers, it is such that quality requirements are not imposed on the area. We do not dispute that parents should be able to choose between a variety of day-to-day services, but we think it is very strange that public crowns should be able to operate care services that do not meet the general educational quality requirements, which we also put forward where our smallest children are taken care of. At the same time, we find it strange that public crowns can be used for one of the most glaring examples of social dumping that we are experiencing in our labour market at all. We have a labour market where the private child care provider is paid according to the number of children. If at present there are only two children, she only gets half the salary. If she gets sick, she doesn't get paid during illness. If she would like to save up for her retirement, she will certainly have to set up a pension scheme herself, because there is no pension scheme whatsoever because she is self-employed. So we move work which has so far been carried out in a quality framework laid down in the legislation and which has been carried out under general contractual conditions to an area where there are no quality requirements and where there are no fixed pay and working conditions. Is it so that we in the Enhedslist are insanely critical of those who are private child carers? No, we're not. We fully understand that there are many who choose it if, for one reason or another, they have had to give up working as municipal day carers. They may have been terminated. We have full respect for the fact that there are people who say: then I must try, even if I no longer have a fixed salary; and although I am no longer paid during illness, it is better than going free. We understand that many do. So the criticism is not directed at the individual private child care provider, but the criticism is directed at legislation in which we pay, through public funds, for the poor quality of care and where we pay for social dumping in this area. That has been my impression, and I think I have seen it before, that I hear a number of arguments that are directed at something that we have not proposed. This is not a proposal that you should not make private day-to-day offers. According to this proposal, private day care institutions can be so well run, private day care is regulated according to a number of other provisions of the legislation. However, with this proposal you cannot have a competitive private offer which, unlike private day care and private institutions, does not have to meet quality requirements and which exposes people to social dumping. Having said that, I am glad that many or most people think that there is something to discuss here. I think there is, too. I would like to say that the proposal that we should have a hearing is, in my view, a fine one, according to the circumstances. I am actually thinking that the Folketing might be ready to take a decision, but I can hear that this matter needs to be clarified, and I think that there will be an understanding of many of the views that I have put forward and which form the basis of this proposal. I think that if we have them thoroughly uncovered in a hearing too, there will be many who, like the Enhedslisten, say that it is probably a problem that we have arrangements for the provision of public funds, where there are no educational quality requirements, and that perhaps it is not so good that we have established a system in which social dumping says sparto to the king of diamonds, in relation to what else we know of social dumping schemes. So I think it is a good thing that we are being prepared to continue the debate, and I must say, as it appears, that I will certainly support in the work of the committee that, when we are unable to proceed as quickly as we hoped in the Enhedslisten, we are discussing it thoroughly together, including hopefully having a hearing where we can all, including undoubtedly the rapporteur for the proposers, learn more about how this area works and how we may nevertheless reach an agreement to regulate, if not quite as we have proposed it, but at least go some way along the way. So I look forward to the further debate.

ParlaMint-DK_20160420003707	The debate is open and I give the floor to the Minister for the Environment and Food. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20160420003733	Thank you for that, President. I would like to thank the Enhedslisten for the six motions for resolutions that have been tabled. Nature is important to us as people and to us as communities here we share recitals with Enhedslisten. But Denmark is a small country, and the government wants a Denmark that has room for nature, for industry and for housing. This requires a clear priority for the various areas in a good balance. The government wants to find solutions where we develop a rich nature and at the same time create the basis for growth in the professions that feed on and with nature. The concern for nature and occupation must go hand in hand, and the balance between use and protection must be found in dialogue with those who use nature on a daily basis. In the government, we believe that nature and agriculture can exist side by side. It's not a question of either or. Farmers are some of our most important nature managers, and we must give them the opportunity to remain so. If we are to make a real difference to nature, we must put the natural effort where it is most relevant. Researchers point out that we can have a huge impact on biodiversity through efforts in forests. I expect action in forests to be an essential element of the future nature package. The government is already making significant efforts to improve the living conditions of animals and plants both on land and on water. I will be presenting later today at midnight, after all, the nature plans for the next 6 years of action in the particularly valuable Natura 2000 areas, where we have allocated 1.8 billion from 2016 to 2021. kr. to the bets. The plans will help to ensure and improve the conditions for internationally protected animal, plant and natural habitats in the areas up to 2021. At the same time as the food and agriculture package ensures better conditions for the agricultural industry, the government is making a targeted effort for the aquatic environment. This is happening, among other things. the restoration of watercourses and the establishment of wetlands. And I expect later this year to present river basin management plans for the period up to 2021. We also make an effort at sea when we follow up Denmark's maritime strategy from 2012. The government is therefore working across the board to ensure a balanced natural and environmental policy that helps to strengthen nature and to meet the EU and UN objectives of halting the decline in biodiversity by 2020. However, as a responsible government for the whole of Denmark, we must insist that the legislation should make room for both the farmer, the production company, the bird-watcher, the fisherman, the children's family, the groomer and everyone else who contributes to and benefits from a society in balance. That is why the government also rejects the six motions for resolutions tabled by the Enhedslisten. B 42 on natural zones: We cannot support the Enhedslisten's proposal to designate so-called natural zones. The Government considers that current protection is sufficient to ensure the right balance between use and protection. A comprehensive restructuring of the nature protection system is therefore not the most cost-effective way to create nature protection. The Government therefore wishes to maintain that Denmark's biodiversity, natural areas and wild plant and animal species are protected by the Nature Conservation Act, while the balance between land interests is covered by the Planning Act. B 43, loss of biodiversity in marine waters: the Enhedslisten's proposal will not allow a balanced use of our marine waters, because it is not enough just to protect, it is a matter of finding a balance that takes account of both the professional use of marine resources and the protection of nature, so that we also have a productive marine environment with high biodiversity in the future. I believe that we basically have the right approach at this stage. We are one of the countries in the European Union that has designated the most marine Natura 2000 sites; 18%. by the Danish sea has been designated as Natura 2000 sites. I would like to emphasise that I fully agree with the Enhedslisten that fisheries must be regulated on the basis of the whole ecosystem. It is precisely the objective of the common fisheries policy that fish stocks should reach a level that can yield maximum sustainable yield. With the food and agriculture package, the regulation of nutrients will in future be targeted at the areas where effort needs are greatest, and here the focus is balanced protection, so that we both take into account agriculture and at the same time ensure that among other things. Our marine environment is not unnecessarily burdened with nutrients. Thus, the government is already addressing many of the challenges identified by Enhedslisten, and I therefore consider that the current efforts are sufficient. B 44, loss of biodiversity in overcrowding and heaths: A number of the efforts and protection measures that Enhedslisten lists in the resolution, I believe, already exist, including extensive care for Natura 2000 sites. The heaths and excessive are protected by Article 3 of the Nature Conservation Act and a significant proportion of these are located within the designated Natura 2000 areas. This means, in practice, that the condition of the land must not be changed. More schemes already exist to create more coherent natural areas. Common to them is that they are based on dialogue and voluntaryness for the pilot owner. This is what we believe to be the best results, both for the pilot and for nature. B 45, loss of biodiversity in fresh meadows and wetlands: the Enhedslisten's proposal for a statutory mitigation requirement I see as an expression of the previous general regulatory model, where the same requirements are made independently of local conditions. It is the government's intention to use targeted regulation to focus future environmental efforts in those areas of the country where the challenges are greatest. Denmark is an agricultural country and must continue to be so, which is why the watercourses must be maintained so that they can ensure a proper derivation of water, but it must of course be done so that at the same time the environment is taken into account. All in all, the one-sided proposals of the Enhedslisten, in which the environment alone is being taken into account, will be in clear contradiction with the government's policy in the field of watercourses. B 46, loss of biodiversity on the coasts: The Government agrees that the free dynamics of the coasts are important, but does not agree with the need for more of Enhedslisten's proposed measures. Compared with several of the initiatives, the government finds that there is already the right balance today. In addition, several of the desired bets already exist. B 47, loss of biodiversity in forests: The Government agrees that forests are essential to the conservation of biodiversity and the government will take a position on forest plantings for biodiversity purposes in the nature package. The Enhedslisten's proposal does not mention where the money for biodiversity forest should come from. For me, it is essential that we allocate forest land cost-effectively so that we get the most biodiversity for the money. The Government believes that there is already a great deal of work to be done on nature and that the existing protection of the natural areas contains to a large extent the opportunities that Enhedslisten is calling for with their six motions for resolutions. We want to safeguard the Danish nature and, as has already been said, will give priority to efforts to halt the decline in biodiversity. And we are also looking at what initiatives should be included in a future nature package that can help to further enhance biodiversity. In general, we in the government believe that efforts for biodiversity must be targeted and cost-effective, and there must be a balance between nature considerations and consideration of the industry. We do not believe that the six motions for resolutions tabled by the Enhedslisten live up to that. With the nature initiatives already under way and with a natural package on the way, the government cannot therefore agree to Enhedslisten's motion for a resolution B 42 to B 47.
