ParlaMint-DK_20160420022050	Thank you to the rapporteur. Then it is the rapporteur for the proposers, Mrs Reumert Gjerding.
ParlaMint-DK_20160420022105	Thank you. I was actually going to say that today is quite special. It would be more fair to say that this night and we must today or tonight, forgive us for discussing how to stop the decline of nature and of our biodiversity, also called biodiversity, that is perhaps more rightful. It is high time that the state of our nature and our species wealth was taken seriously, because Denmark becomes poorer every day that passes; poorer because the diversity of nature disappears at an alarming rate before our eyes. The decline in biodiversity and the destruction of nature is not something that just happens in a distant rainforest thousands of kilometres away; the loss takes place just outside our own door. Right out there, we lose a diversity and wealth that is the prerequisite for our life base and that is so crucial to our quality of life. I think that most politicians who are just peripherally concerned with this area know very well that it is mad. We know very well that countless political declarations of intent have never been followed up by the necessary action. We all know very well that back in 2010 with a Left Minister in the lead, our agreement to the UN Convention on Biodiversity was reaffirmed with the very promise that we, together with the rest of the world, would stop the decline in biodiversity in our own nature by 2020. Today we are writing 2016 and we must honestly say that we are infinitely far from achieving that goal. The situation is that 1 in 5 of the approx. 32,000 known Danish species are on the brink of extinction. In virtually all our natural habitats, whether they are meadows, exaggerations, seas, heaths, shores and forests etc. is nature under severe pressure. It may sound very abstract, but let us take some very concrete examples: Not so many years ago there were several thousand pairs of breeding owls in Denmark. Today there are between 50 and 60 pairs left. The church owl could be the next species to disappear. Not many years ago, several types of butterflies could be seen in forest clearings. Today we have only one of these species of forest clearing left, namely reddish mother-of-pearl butterfly. The others have disappeared, imperceptibly one by one. Not too many years ago you could find the beautiful orchid hill barking herb on many meadows in Denmark; today you can only find it at three locations. Not many years ago, meadow plum was an ordinary flower, everyone knew. Most people today do not know what the flower looks like, because you have to be exceptionally lucky to find a copy. That's how you could go on. Our country becomes unnoticedly poorer every day. This development must have been reversed, and that is what is so positive, namely that there is a broad consensus on it here in the Danish Parliament. I have not yet heard any party say that Denmark should abandon its adherence to the Biodiversity Convention or have any hope of failing to meet the objectives that the European Union has also set, namely that we must stop the decline in a few years' time. That is why, with these motions for resolutions, we want us to start discussing in concrete terms what needs to be done, so that not only do we talk about the ambitious objectives that we are all so much in agreement on, but we also talk about the fact that this task is extremely important. Because we are so intensive an agricultural country, because nature is squeezed out of the open country and lies only as small incoherent spots, because our forestry is primarily about logging and timber production, and because our marine areas are first and foremost used for fishing, nature has a problem, then it does not have its own place and it is not being able to develop and it is being pushed more and more into the defensive. And that is why we see the decline that I have already explained. To stop this decline is the purpose of Enhedslisten's six motions for resolutions, i.e. this natural package. What is central, and this is not something we have come up with, is that we are leaning on extensive research and on what Danish experts say, namely that nature needs more space. Nature needs areas where the primary purpose is not agriculture, but where nature may be served. It is not timber production where nature may be taken into account; it is not fishing where nature may be taken into account. No, these are areas where nature has its own right, where nature must thrive, and where species must also be able to be so. And that is why we have tabled this proposal. As I mentioned earlier, it is not something entirely new in Danish politics. This was, in fact, something that the Socialists mentioned as far back as 2011, namely to introduce a natural zone into the planning law, where the main objective was nature and nothing else. Then we have mentioned various things in the debate, and I would like to repeat how we are going to finance it here, because there is no doubt that it costs some money to look after our nature and ensure that our children and grandchildren also have a rich nature and can experience the same species as we do and can also experience larch songs, and that the vibe announces spring, because it is species that are in severe decline. This requires that we dare to set aside some land for nature and not for production. We're financing 350 million. kr. In our own Finance Act you can go in to find it online and then redispose 150 million. kr. from the proceeds of pesticide taxes for natural purposes. Then the rural programme can also be used for certain natural purposes, not for untouched forests, but for actions in the open country. Here we re-prioritize in the heading of growth and competitiveness, which has been allocated an average of 300 million. kr. in the coming years. In addition, we want to transfer the full modulation that can be transferred from pillar one to pillar two in the agricultural support schemes and use the money we transfer there to support nature. The rapporteur of the Conservatives then said that this would lead to quite unreasonable restrictions on agricultural production. I do not know whether these were exactly the words, but I think that we should also bear in mind all the different professions which are deeply dependent on the good nature of nature, which will be taken into account with such a natural package. This is among other things. fishing in the long term. If we manage to deal with our fisheries resources, then there will be more fish in the future. It is a tourism business where tourists will come and experience our rich nature. So the rapporteur of the Left said that we have already done things, and he asked: what have we not done well enough? I think that what we have not done well enough is that we have not done enough at all. And it has to be said that nature is uncompromising and it cannot be negotiated with. Whatever we think we have done some nature recovery projects here and there, it is obviously not enough because the decline continues. And that is where you have to continue until you are in the finish line, even if you think it is unreasonable, even if you think it is difficult. The nature we need to protect does not depend on what I had with the rapporteur of the Danish People's Party and what our personal preferences are, i.e. whether we think it is nice to go for a walk in an untouched natural forest, or whether we prefer a forest that is driven for good, where the trees stand as matches ready to become the plank floors of the future. We know what nature we have in Denmark; we know what it is we are obliged to protect. This is not something we should be making up or discussing. Whether we can make it then is the question, but we have to get started now. Thank you for the floor.

ParlaMint-DK_20160420025642	Then we have finished with the rapporteurs and then we go on to the rapporteur for the authors of the motion, Mrs Maria Reumert Gjerding.
ParlaMint-DK_20160420025708	Thank you. The two proposals we are now considering are B 43, proposals for a parliamentary resolution to stop the loss of biodiversity in Denmark's marine waters, and B 46, proposals for a parliamentary resolution to stop the loss of biodiversity on Denmark's coasts. Denmark has more than 100,000 km 2 The majority of Denmark's surface area is thus hidden under water, and the problems experienced and experienced by the seas over time have largely avoided public attention. Political attention has mainly been limited to isolated serious incidents such as oxygen loss, storm tide or fish stock collapse, and unfortunately the problems of the sea are often only debated to the extent that there are obvious economic consequences related to business interests. But nature in the sea is under pressure and marine diversity is in decline, and this is due to a large number of man-made influences. This is because we emit more nitrogen than nature can handle, because fishing is extremely intensive, and this is because the seabed is being towed almost as intensively as the land is ploughed. But also the extraction of raw materials and the discharge of environmental foreign substances are everyday in the sea and help to put the portrait under serious pressure. Despite our political agreement on the protection of biodiversity, there is a general decline, and I think we will have to deal with this when we are so close to the goal we should have worked towards for very many years, which we have reaffirmed and promised each other back in 2010, and since then it has only gone the wrong way in all of Denmark's natural habitats. To some extent, it must call for thought in this House. In relation to the sea, we see that eelgrass and seaweed belts no longer grow in as deep water as before. Algeum blooms due to nitrogen pollution cause oxygen loss, and since the mid-1990s the diversity of bottom-living animals in parts of Kattegat has about halved. More stocks of fish have been drastically reduced and some have almost completely disappeared, just as more populations of breeding and overwintering birds are on strong and worrying declines. What is important in this context is that Denmark is important for a large number of European migratory birds dependent on the Danish coastal waters, it is simply here that they find food, and those species have also shown a worrying decline in recent years as our nature no longer thrives. If we are also to have a healthy marine environment in the future to support the fish stocks on which the fishermen of the future must live and which can support a rich nature rich in experiences and a bird's life in a special international class, we need to focus our attention on portrait. We need to deal with how we deal with a fishery whose over-exploitation of resources has destroyed portland and depleted stocks. We have to deal with how we deal with agricultural pressures on portland through the excessive nutrient emissions and the extraction of raw materials, which include: destroy important spawning areas. What I think is important to say is that while in the Enhedslisten we absolutely believe that nature has a right in itself, nature is also what supports a large number of other professions, which we must also take into account all those professions that depend on a clean nature and ecosystems that are connected. It is about tourism, for example. on fishing. When we in the Enhedslisten have tabled this motion for a resolution, B 43, to stop the loss of marine biodiversity, it is because we believe that it is about to be the last call in terms of launching the initiatives to make sure that we just reach the target by 2020. In our proposal, we have tried to be so close to what the researchers and experts not only generally in nature and biodiversity, but also those who are actually experts in the Danish nature and biodiversity on the coasts and at portrait point to the need. What they are saying is that we need to set up large, coherent marine national parks where a particular type of fishing can no longer take place, and that is fishing with bottom-up gear. You say that we need to reduce agricultural nitrogen emissions because our vulnerable coastal nature cannot cope with it. This may seem unreasonable, because in Germany they have a different nature, so there they have to derive more, but there we only have a different and much more vulnerable nature in Denmark. They also point out that we must take action against a wide range of environmentally dangerous substances. Furthermore, we will need to take action against mining in particular areas, especially where there are important habitats, that is, where fish stocks depend on the nature being very good, so that they can reproduce themselves and so that there can be strong coherent fish stocks in the future as well. I would like to thank the parties for their consideration of B 43. I still think we are a step away from having a dialogue about what we can actually meet about. In fact, I think it seems a little absurd that we say to each other that we agree on the objectives, but that we cannot start to have a dialogue on how we can also agree on the means across the Folketing. In relation to biodiversity on the coasts, the second motion for a resolution we are debating here, namely B 46 to halt the decline in biodiversity on the coasts of Denmark, I think it is very important to state that Denmark's coasts are absolutely unique in the international context. It is the coasts that make up most of Denmark's wild and unspoiled nature, and it is a very special natural feature of Denmark, which is valued incredibly high, both by Danes and by tourists. Together with the shallow coastal areas, which are also very special for Denmark, the beaches and coastal dunes contain a large and indeed in a European context a unique biological diversity, which we in Denmark have a very special responsibility to protect. The Danish beach area has been reduced by more than 50%. within the last few hundred years. This is mainly due to the fact that we have taken a great many of these lands in production. Despite this, Denmark contains approx. 36% of the total area of beaches throughout Europe, and it says that we have something very special here, something that people come here to experience, but also that it is something so unique that we have a very special responsibility to protect it. A similar picture accounts for our coastal dunes, where we also have 84 per cent in Denmark. of the total area of the dune in Europe. And again, that is why it is so worrying that precisely the natural habitat types that exist in our country and where we have a responsibility and where we have something that is special international are in such a serious decline, we have to respond to and we have to stop the damage before it is too late. Motion for a resolution B 46 is about taking that responsibility seriously, and we must listen to the prominent biodiversity scientists who very specifically point out that what we need to ensure is varied grazing on the remaining beaches. We need to convert coastal agricultural land from cultivation to nature-based grazing and drainage, so that nature can get the land back. We do not get around the fact that one of the main reasons why we have such major problems with our nature and biodiversity in Denmark is that we have too large areas under ploughs, and these are areas we have to take back from agriculture and surrender to nature. Thank you for the floor.

This is also due to the fact that there are no longer animals on the land, so that trees grow, they simply jump in the forest, and then they remove the basis of living for precisely the particular species that exist on the heaths. It has gone the same way with excessive. When nature is in decline, the same way goes with the insects, the butterflies, the flowers and the plants that are associated with exactly the nature type. Half of our bumble bee species are gone, and also a lot of our butterfly species, such as... Black-spotted Bluebird and Hedeple Force have either disappeared or greatly decreased in number. And excessive with a multitude of floral species is mostly replaced by uniform grain fields. So to put it this way: excessive and heat are very endangered habitats, which have undergone a very severe land decline in Denmark over the last 200 years. Apart from being threatened by nitrogen pollution and lack of grazing, these habitat types are also threatened by fragmentation, as it is called, simply because the areas have become so small and are so scattered between each other that if the rare animals that are there first disappear, they will not be able to re-immigrate from somewhere else. The good thing about these nature proposals, and which are actually repeated in all of them, is that we know very well what it takes to stop the decline. We know that nitrogen pollution must be reduced. Some have just passed a legislative proposal which goes in the opposite direction. It causes more problems here and now for that nature. We also need more coherent natural areas and we need land to be grazed. These may be farm animals, as we have said in the past, but wild grazing will also be obvious. This is something that you start so small in several places in Denmark, among others. on Bornholm and in Store Vildmose. But this is about creating the best conditions for the free dynamics of nature, as they call it, so that the free, natural processes that make nature thrive unfold. In fact, we just lack the political will to give nature that place. In relation to meadows and wetlands, I would also like to speak a little in honour of the rapporteur of the Danish People's Party about the marvellous nature that exists there, because I do not think we should have many decades back in time until everyone knew the vibe's song. When the vibes arrived in February or March, it was known that spring was coming. It's not like that today. Today most people do not know the vibe's song, and if there were enough vibes, even the children at Nørrebro should have the opportunity to come out and experience them. When the spring response of the vibe stands as a weak peep compared to past times, it is because the vibes and lark and all the other species belonging to the open country no longer have very many places to live. The biodiversity, our all together, Denmark's species wealth of amazing, special species that help create and define our country and have been so for decades, is razed down. They disappear when their habitats disappear and when nature is generally in decline. Why is nature so? After all, we have discussed this a few times during the night. It is because agriculture has taken up very large parts of our area. When we lay out large parts of the area for agriculture, there is less room for other things, and then nature suffers. There's a perfectly natural connection, we can't have it all. Wetlands and meadows have been drained and converted into biological deserts in the form of endless wheat fields, and there the animals cannot live, there the birds cannot find food, and there is nothing to do for a butterfly. The watercourses have been accomplished, they have been piped, they have been deepened. Our watercourses have become drainage channels for agriculture. If there are no aquatic plants, if there are no shallow spawning grounds, then there are no fish, then life is not as simple as that. As I have now repeated a number of times, we must stop the decline in nature and biodiversity, and we really have quite a few years to do so. And we have to do this for several reasons: we have to do this, among other things. do so if we also want our children and grandchildren to experience the nature that we ourselves have experienced. If they are also to experience the song of the vibes and the larch, if they are also to see the butterflies, which are so distinctive and so special for Denmark, if they are also to experience the characteristic flowering sea of the meadows and the violet heather of the heath, then we must do something now. Then some of the so-called low bottom areas will have to return to nature; then we will have to get the animals out of the stables and into nature, because nature needs them to care for itself, otherwise it will grow and be destroyed. We must also dare to bet on the wild grass-eaters in the form of bisons, wild horses and crown animals that can support nature in ways that farm animals cannot. Then we need to get the water out of the pipes, we need to stop drainage, and we need to give the water back to the natural cycle. We need to stop porridge cutting. We must recreate the winding and winding nature in the watercourses both for the sake of nature and also for the sake of recreational fishing. At least we have to make some choices. Where are the rivers most optimally dewatering agriculture, and where are we going to give the streams back to nature? Because it's an illusion to think that we can have both. The solutions to biodiversity problems are, in a sense, too over-exploited, heath, meadows and wetland areas. In fact, the only thing that is lacking is the political will to get started. Thank you for the floor.

They felt that there was a need for more evaluation, more knowledge of what specific types of red-listed species were to be preserved in their area. And it was certainly an eye-opener for me. I hope that, in the context of the nature package negotiations, we can have a discussion on how private forest owners can also enter into, although it has to be said that this is much more difficult. I mean, the easy solution is simply to use the state's own forests, but I also think we need to listen to the reports that point to the fact that this is just not necessarily the way we get the most biodiversity for money. It was just one such consideration here at the end of the evening. But we do not support this resolution.

ParlaMint-DK_20160420042037	Thank you very much, and I would like to start by thanking you very much for a very long debate that has been going on all night. The last proposal here in our nature and biodiversity package is about forests, and if you have followed the political debate over the last year, you can see that there has actually been a large degree of consensus in both the red and the blue blocs that action has to be taken here. Socially-economically, nature and biodiversity are one of the best areas to do, and it is incredibly gratifying that we have an area on which we agree. And there is also good reason to put our forests into action. For in the most recent report to the European Union on our internationally protected natural areas, researchers found that the conservation status of forests was 100%. - - - - - - - - - - - ~ - ~ - ~ - ~ - ~ - ~ - ~ - ~ - ~ - ~ - ~ - ~ - ~ - ~ - ~ - ~ - ~ - ~ - ~ - ~ - ~ - ~ - ~ - ~ - ~ - ~ Translated into Danish, this means that biodiversity and nature in the forests are feeling really, really bad. So it is absolutely crucial to do something in the forests to stop the decline in nature and biodiversity, and this is basically linked to Denmark being originally a forest-covered country. So many of our species are of course there, and there are very many forests, because society has been asking for timber from the forests for years. And that is why many of the forests are uniform: they are the same varieties, the trees are the same age, and they stand there in rows that are quite straight trunks and will be perfect for planks, for floors and for building materials, which we also need. But it's not good for nature. There is a great deal of our nature in the forests, which depend on real old trees, which depend on dead trees, and which depend on a great variety of different types of wood and wood, and that is why it is so difficult, not to say almost impossible, to reconcile consideration for the most rare and vulnerable species in the forest with timber production, and that is why we are gradually agreed that we should deal with the most vulnerable and endangered species, we will have to set the forest aside for untouched forest. But setting the chainsaw is not enough. We also need to restore the forest's natural water cycle. Now I have discussed ditches earlier with the rapporteur of the Danish People's Party, and it is precisely the ditches that we will have to throw to, and we must stop draining the forests so that the water can rise and stand high again, and precisely create the natural lightnings and natural dynamics that are found originally in the forests. Then we will have to get some of the original herbivores back, and we have also discussed this a few times today. This is particularly about some of the larger and wild animals, which create a great and natural dynamic in the forests, on which they also depend. In the past, the forest was characterized by grazing animals. Unfortunately, the peace forest regulation came to an end as far back as 1805, and that is what we have to do and touch over and over again to allow us to get wild animals into the forests.
